<p>🔧 Bash: cd <span class="redacted">redacted: private</span>; python3 - &lt;&lt;'EOF' import sys; sys.path.insert(0,'tools/commenter'… (Check real comments under the new logic, fix 'ninety', upda…)</p>
<p>✅ 39 lines, 2116 chars — ## draft/book-01/scenes/02-a-war-horse-on-a-milk-cart.md /   moved     L15-&gt;17  "a second weather," /   moved     L47-&gt;49  "as if he'd ruled something out."</p>

<div class="assistant-prose">
<p>Fixed, tested, and committed (`51f5e10`); server restarted.</p>
<p>**The bug:** the stored context was only consulted when the quote occurred more than once, and then only as an exact 48-character match. A short quote with a single hit anywhere else was accepted as "moved" with no context check; multiple hits with any nearby edit fell through to "nearest to the old offset". Fuzzy matches ignored context entirely.</p>
<p>**The fix:** every non-exact step now scores candidates by how much stored context survives around them — counted character by character outward from the quote, so an edit right beside the selection costs a lot and one far away costs little — and orphans rather than guesses when the best candidate keeps too little. Distance from the old offset only breaks ties. Context stored per new comment is 160 characters each side. Legacy anchors with no context at all resolve only on a unique hit of a quote ≥12 characters.</p>
<p>**Regression tests** in `tools/commenter/test_relocate.py` (7 cases: deleted short quote must orphan, not jump; moved short quote found by context past a lookalike; lightly edited context still picks the right occurrence; legacy long quotes; exact; context width). All pass; two failed before the fix.</p>
<p>**On your real comments:** the twelve you left on scene 2 whose lines I rewrote now show as `orphaned` (correct — the text is gone), the three on surviving lines resolve as `moved` to the right places, and the groundwork's comments are all still `exact`. Before the fix, "You" and "clever" were landing on other Yous and clevers.</p>
<p>Also in that commit: the lens's one new finding, *"the year he turned ninety"* → *"two hundred"*, in scene 1.</p>
</div>

# EDITING REPORT — scenes 01 and 02, read as one span<br>
<br>
**Mechanical counts (2,284 words):** em-dashes 3, of which 2 are the two title headers — **one em-dash in the actual prose** (Edwin's interruption at 02 L85). Under budget, but see F14. "the whole of" ×2 (watch-list). "honest" ×1, the exact retired phrase. "half past" ×3 in 2,284 words.<br>
<br>
---<br>
<br>
## PASS 1 — SENSE (a hostile literal reader)<br>
<br>
**F1. 02 L17 — "something worn close over the whole person, and moving, slowly, like light on water that is not itself moving."**<br>
Does not survive being said flat. "Moving… like light on water that is not itself moving" — the relative clause's antecedent is ambiguous (light or water?), and either reading contradicts the main clause. Two flat versions, cannot choose: llm-tells' test says cut, don't repair the phrasing.<br>
→ *"something worn close over her, moving slowly, the way light moves on still water."*<br>
<br>
**F2. 02 L83 — "and he knew the shape of it: the argument that had gone wrong at the root."**<br>
Direct hit on the family DRMacIver banned by name two days ago ("You use phrases like 'the whole shape of it' a lot and it's a weird phrase that means very little and I don't like it"). <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><br>
→ *"and he knew where it had gone wrong. It had gone wrong at the root."*<br>
<br>
**F3. 02 L77 — "I have helped some parishes, one at a time, which is a different size of thing"**<br>
Same family: "a different size of thing" gestures at weight and names nothing. Also an evaluative trailing "which" clause (zero budget) inside quotation marks.<br>
→ *"I have helped some parishes, one at a time. Saving the world doesn't describe anything I've done."* (<span class="redacted">redacted: place</span> also removes one of the speech's three "and"-joined halves.)<br>
<br>
**F4. 02 L39/43/47 — the loaf, "what the loaf was for."**<br>
"I don't know what the loaf was for" and "He knew what the loaf was for" do not parse: the loaf is an image in a vision, and images are not *for* things. She means she can't read it; he means he knows who the woman is. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> In ch. 001 the antecedent was "most lives saved," a quantity, and the sentence worked. Here the reference chain is broken.<br>
→ Peri: *"I can't make the loaf fit. I've had it three days."* (drop "I don't know what the loaf was for" — the next sentence already says it). Edwin: *"He knew who the woman with the loaf was. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><br>
<br>
**F5. 02 L39 — "One thing a day from the Weave, everything else from anybody who'd stand still, and put them together."**<br>
"and put them together" hangs off no subject and no verb it can share. The reader re-reads.<br>
→ *"One thing a day from the Weave, everything else from anybody who'd stand still. Then you put them together."*<br>
<br>
**F6. 02 L39 — "A man writing in a book, with the window on his left." She looked at the window, which was on his left.**<br>
<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> **They are in the kitchen.** The man-writing-in-a-book image is the *study* (scene 01: "in plain view of the study window", "three columns into arranging it"). The kitchen window being also on his left is coincidence, and nothing in the scene has established which way he is facing at the table. Worse: she names three images — hill, **beans in jars**, man at a window — and the kitchen inventory at 02 L3 (herbs, dresser, catalogues, sampler) contains no beans in jars, so two of her three confirmations go unpaid on the page.<br>
→ Either put the beans in jars into L3's inventory and let *that* be what she looks at (self-verifying, in the right room, and funnier), or cut the "she looked at the window" beat.<br>
<br>
**F7. 02 L3 — "A dresser of crockery that did not match, because thirty years of parishes had each sent a piece."**<br>
Two problems. (a) Agreement: "thirty years… had each sent a piece" — years cannot each send a piece. (b) Continuity inside the same scene: L61 has him working for "three hundred years" and L17 "three hundred and fifty years". Thirty years of parish gifts contradicts the man the scene is establishing.<br>
→ *"A dresser of crockery, no two pieces alike, because parishes send things."*<br>
<br>
**F8. 02 L3 — "Seed catalogues in a stack going back further than she had been alive."**<br>
Doesn't parse ("further than she had been alive"). ch. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><br>
→ *"A stack of seed catalogues older than she was."*<br>
<br>
**F9. 02 L59/79/81 — cup vs mug.** "She had the grace to look at her cup" (L59), then "her mug in both hands" (L79), "She set the mug down" (L81). Same vessel, two names, twenty lines apart. Pick one; the exact continuity class DRMacIver flagged in the ch. 001/002 margins.<br>
<br>
**F10. 02 L11 — "He gave her three, and found he had counted them."**<br>
<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> Two faults: the register is stage-direction flat, so the beat arrives spent; and the proposition is thin — she *said* three, so counting them is what anyone does. If the intent is "he was already paying her more attention than he wanted to," that isn't in the sentence.<br>
→ *"He gave her three, which was one more than he had meant to be paying attention."* — or cut the clause and let "He gave her three" stand plain.<br>
<br>
**F11. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><br>
Jukebox test fails on the scene's own material: Edwin *is* the farmer, and reading skies is his literal paid job in the paragraph above. The comparison compares him to himself.<br>

<br>
Mostly a conversation, and the spine of scene 02 is genuinely two people: Edwin's corrections take up Peri's actual words, and her theory eats each refutation, so every turn changes the state of the argument.<br>

**Scene 01 — `draft/book-01/scenes/01-pellings-half-past-twelve-soft.md`**<br>
<br>
1. "Edwin was three columns into arranging it"<br>
Flat: he had completed three columns of the arrangement. Two candidate readings and I can't choose: columns of figures/entries in his ledger (supported by the next paragraph, which is all writing and ruling lines), or columns of air in the weather-working (supported by "arranging it" = the rain). Neither is cashed anywhere. And as a progress measure "three columns into" is empty either way — the reader learns no quantity and no stage.<br>
<br>
2. "Most people used the time to reconsider. The post did not come up at all; the post left things with the widow Crane at the bottom. The parishes wrote. The ten o'clock class came early and waited in the class garden."<br>
The inference doesn't hold. The claim is *people who start up the hill turn back*; the three examples are of people who never start (the post), people who don't come at all (parishes), and people who do come and do not turn back (the class). Not one instance of reconsidering. The paragraph asserts X and then illustrates not-X.<br>
<br>
3. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><br>
Flat: they need the rain held until the shearing is done. But it is two days of shearing, and the rain lands at 12:30 on one of them — which soaks the flock waiting for day two. "On account of the shearing" is offered as the reason for the timing and does not survive being made explicit: the stated reason argues for no rain at all, not for rain at half twelve. Whatever the real reason is, this sentence isn't it.<br>
<br>
4. "If he took hold of it this morning he could walk it inland overnight … and bring what was left over the Pellings at half past twelve."<br>
Flat: taken this morning, walked overnight, it arrives at 12:30 — necessarily tomorrow's 12:30. But the scene reads throughout as the rain being *today's* job ("a sea-front off the coast waiting for a man who had work to do", the note filed as *Pellings, 12:30, soft* with no date, the pressure of the morning). The sentence's own arithmetic and the scene's urgency point at different days; a reader cannot tell which is intended.<br>
<br>
5. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><br>
Flat: she is matching the tower to an image the Weave gave her. Given what scene 02 says the Weave actually showed — a hill with the road in bends, a room with beans in jars, a man writing with the window on his left — she has no tower to check it against. She has the *hill* and, once she sees the window, the *man*. As written, the gesture cashes nothing she has.<br>
<br>
6. "If it's Upper Wold about the fourteenth, I haven't decided, and I've said so twice."<br>
The count has no anchor. The only evidence on the page is a note saying "You said you would decide" — one prior exchange, and one in which he said the opposite of "I haven't decided". The exact construction the author flagged: a specific tally the scene has not earned.<br>
<br>
7. "you are destined to help me save the world" (Peri, at the door)<br>
Flat: the Weave told her Edwin's role is to help her save the world. Her own account in scene 02 falsifies this twice over. She asked "who can *help* me save the world" and was shown **a woman with a bandage** — not this hill. The hill came from "who is the best person to *teach me magic*" and "who can *show me how* to save the world". So the one question whose wording matches her doorstep claim returned somebody else. Her opening line misreports her own evidence, and nothing in either scene marks that as a mistake she is making.<br>
<br>
**Scene 02 — `draft/book-01/scenes/02-a-war-horse-on-a-milk-cart.md`**<br>
<br>
8. "It showed me three things. A hill with the road going up it in bends. A room with beans in jars. A man writing in a book, with the window on his left." / "One thing a day from the Weave" / "Four days of answers"<br>
The images don't reconcile with the budget. One question a day, and she accounts for four days: teach-me-magic (hill), who-can-help (bandage woman), who-am-I-to-fight (nothing), and the third go (churchyard, then hill). Under that ledger the beans-in-jars and the man-writing images were never delivered by any question she describes — and the hill is the *only* thing the answering questions produced. Three images from a one-image-a-day oracle across four accounted days, two of which produced other things entirely.<br>
<br>
9. "Then *who am I to fight*. Nothing at all."<br>
Flat: the Weave, which answers true, reports that she has no opponent. That is a substantive true answer, not a null — it says the premise of her quest is empty — and neither she nor Edwin treats it as anything. She frowns at the churchyard instead. A day's true answer saying "no enemy" sits in the middle of a scene about whether there is an enemy, and does no work.<br>
<br>
10. "Both times it had looked the same: something worn close over the whole person, moving slowly … On the Seer it had fitted like a good coat."<br>
"Both times the same" and then a property attributed to only one of them, immediately followed by "heavier stuff than the Seer's had been", which requires the Seer's to be a distinguishable weight. The sameness claim is retracted by the next two sentences before it has done anything.<br>
<br>
11. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><br>
The two "it"s are not the same thing. The first is a quantity of substance (a great deal of fate magic / of whatever she is); the second is a course or career (at the start of it). Flat restatement has to pick, and the sentence needs both.<br>
<br>
12. "That's three things agreeing," she said. … [three items] … "And I asked the Weave who could show me how to save the world, and it showed me this hill."<br>
She announces three and delivers four in one unbroken speech — the fourth being the strongest one. If the miscount is meant as character, nothing marks it; as written it reads as the author losing the tally mid-paragraph. Compare the flagged "twice you've said farmer".<br>
<br>
13. "That's four things I've brought you, and you've said no to all four."<br>
False on the page. Between her four-item speech and this line, Edwin does not say no to anything — he proposes she change the question she asks the Weave. Earlier he denied being an archmage-weapon, which is one refusal, not four, and it predates three of the four items. The listener's understanding ("you have rejected each of my four proofs") is not what happened.<br>
<br>
14. "if it shows you a woman with a bandage again, then you and I are going to sit here and talk about what the word *save* actually—"<br>
He has just instructed her to stop asking *who* and ask *what am I to save the world from*. A what-question cannot plausibly return "a woman with a bandage again" — the "again" points back at an answer to a who-question. And if it somehow did, the lesson he threatens ("what *save* actually means") doesn't follow from a danger-image; it followed from the *helper* image. The conditional and its consequence are wired to the previous question, not the one he just set.&lt;/result&gt;<br>

Because Y* is negative parallelism, banned as a construction and banned inside quotation marks by dialogue rule 3. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> (c) It is a characterisation regression from the reference dialogue: ch. 001 has *"You argue really well. That's the problem. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> The rewrite makes her a cool tactician appraising an opponent. `reader-notes-rewrite.md` §2 is explicitly about not sanding this off.<br>
Proposed: *"I want you to know I was listening. You argue well. That's the trouble. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> And the world's more important than an argument."*<br>
<br>
**2. L31 — "Not to the world. To me, and to the ten parishes out that window."**<br>
Edwin's emotional peak. Negative parallelism again, and balanced halves — `edwin.md` 4 names balanced halves at a peak as a **defect**. It is also his *second* contrastive pair in the scene (L35, "You did not try asking. You announced."), and rule 13 allows one, which should be L35's, the better line.<br>
Proposed: drop the antithesis. *"Do you understand what you've done? Not to the world. To the ten parishes out that window."* still has it; better is *"Do you understand what you've done to the ten parishes out that window?"* and let the speech run.<br>
<br>
**3. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><br>
The most portable sentence in the speech is its last sentence. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> It is also a triad-with-swerve: three parish facts, then the fourth item turns. Note the *word*→*line* substitution is a genuine fix to the author's ch. 001 objection; the defect is placement, not wording. Proposed: move it up — put the boots-and-pending sentence before the sea-front, and end on the sheep, which is a fact with no shape on it.<br>
<br>
**4. L25 "Undo it." and L41 "Get off my hill."**<br>
Both bare imperatives, both Edwin's, both at hinges. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> I flag this as a **rule collision rather than a demand**: both lines are on the page in ch. 001, which is the dialogue the author liked. If either goes, "Undo it." is the cheaper one — his declarative form would be *"That knot doesn't stay."* — and "Get off my hill" should stay whatever the file says.<br>
<br>
**5. Peri has no tally anywhere in the scene.**<br>
<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> She is refused from L41 onward and there is not one. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> This is the largest character-voice hole in the scene.<br>
<br>
**6. L37 — Peri over the "and" cap.**<br>
<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> 001, so inheritance is not a defence. Proposed: *"And then whatever's coming would have come. You'd have been here doing weather. Everyone would die, and it would be my fault for being bad at arguing."*<br>
(L33 sits exactly at two — at the cap, not over.)<br>
<br>
**7. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> Rule 6a satisfied. But there is none at L5, her highest-pressure line, where she is instead perfectly fluent and epigrammatic — see finding 1.<br>
<br>
---<br>
<br>
### B. Physical beats at the peak (named rule, `edwin.md` 11)<br>
<br>
**8. L31–L37 runs four turns with no qualifying beat between them.**<br>
<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> L33's "Whiter, and her chin up, and not moving an inch" is a state, not a chain of acts. L35 ("You did not try asking. You announced.") is **entirely bare and untagged**. L37 opens on "That got a flicker of her own indignation" — an evaluative clause, which the rule excludes by name. So the emotional centre of the scene has one posture note and one emotion gloss holding it up. Recommend giving L35 hands: he is standing over the table with two cups on it; twenty-one words on what he does with one of them, then the neutral line.<br>
<br>
**9. Related continuity slip: Peri's cup vanishes.** She has it in both hands at the end of scene 02; at L23 she is "sitting very straight with both hands flat on the table" with no beat putting it down. This is the exact class of slip the author flagged in ch. 001 ("cold cup of tea").<br>
<br>
---<br>
<br>
### C. Retired forms, verdict clauses, self-annotation<br>
<br>
**10. L3 — "the way they do when a person is reading something that is not in the room."**<br>
The "in the way/manner of a person who" family, retired outright (`voices.md` pattern 2). <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> Cut the clause.<br>
<br>
**11. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><br>
Windup relative clause on a narration beat (budget one per 3,500 words — and L47 spends the other), fake-archaic register ("he who"), and it is the narrator underlining the irony for the reader. Proposed: *"He had watched her do it, and taken it for a girl finding her nerve."*<br>
<br>
**12. L3 — "looking for the moment he should have seen."** You miss a *sign*, not a moment; ch. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> Near-idiom (pattern 5).<br>
<br>
**13. L27 — the pamphlet sentence.** Sixty words, and it does four things at once. "She said it as if reading it off a page, **and she was**" is a device stated and then confirmed — self-annotation. "for people who had not expected to be holding any" is a type-of-person clause. "and she had plainly read the pamphlet, and could recite…" is polysyndeton ending on a verdict. **Tense slip:** "the church printed" reads as though it has stopped; "prints" is meant. "currently sitting" is a memo-register word (pattern 11). The *content* is a good answer to the author's ch. 001 note ("What rulebook?") and should survive; the sentence should not.<br>
Proposed: *"The church prints its restrictions on fate magic in a pamphlet, for people who did not expect to be holding any. She had read it. She could recite the part about bindings to the man one was sitting in."* — and see finding 19 on whether that last clause survives the aphorism budget.<br>
<br>
**14. L39 — "He put the kettle back on the hob, which did not need doing."**<br>
Evaluative trailing "which", zero budget. Proposed: let the act carry it — *"He put the kettle back on the hob. It had water in it already."*<br>
<br>
**15. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><br>
Narrator inferring motive, and it explains the joke ch. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> Cut from "and she must have judged".<br>
<br>
**16. L13 — "and neither of those was any use, because there was no moment at which the thing was coming and had not yet arrived."**<br>
Three-item "and" chain ending on a verdict (pattern 7), and the *because* clause is the joke's rhythm without the joke's logic: flat-restated it means "there was no warning", which the sentence spends nineteen words arriving at sideways. ch. 001's "the working was not aimed at him until it had already happened" is the same idea and parses on first read. Proposed: *"He was three hundred and fifty years old and held more mana than anyone living. Neither was any use. The working was not aimed at him until it had already happened."*<br>
<br>
**17. L29 — "because he had a rule about frightened decisions."**<br>
This is the repair of a line the author objected to in ch. 001, and it is better, but the compression still makes the reader supply the missing word. Proposed: *"because he made no decisions while he was frightened."*<br>
<br>
**18. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><br>
"Like that" points at nothing the sentence has named. This is the banned family from the 2026-08-29 entry — a sentence shaped like weight with a hand-wave inside. Cut.<br>
<br>
---<br>
<br>
### D. Aphorism budget and figures<br>
<br>
**19. Three narrator aphorisms; budget is one.**<br>
(a) L27 "could recite the part about bindings to a man in whose chest one was currently sitting"; (b) L39 "Not a pain. A fact."; (c) L47 "who had heard *reassuring* and nothing on either side of it". Keep (a) — it is stuck to its particulars and pays off the pamphlet. (b) is also negative parallelism, a fragment closer, and the narrator stating the theme, at the scene's emotional centre where the irony is supposed to step back; recommend cutting both sentences and ending the paragraph on "The knot behind his breastbone sat there and did nothing." ("did nothing" of the knot is inside its rate here, and it is news.) (c) — see finding 21.<br>
<br>
**20. L13 — "It was a hand closing."**<br>
The scene's only figure, and it is not grown from the scene's material: the material is rope — slack cabling, loops, a hawser. A hand closing is imported. It fails the jukebox test ("what is it doing for *this* moment that the rope doesn't"). Recommend cutting; "What it felt like afterwards was a knot" follows the rope image directly.<br>
<br>
---<br>
<br>
### E. The last two lines<br>
<br>
**21. L47 — "said Peri, who had heard *reassuring* and nothing on either side of it, and went out."**<br>
This correctly fixes the author's continuity complaint (in ch. 001 "reassuring" was the last word she could have stopped listening at), but the replacement is a coinage, not an idiom — "heard X and nothing on either side of it" is not English, and the reader has to reconstruct "caught the word, missed the sentence". Near-idiom, the most damaging category per instance. It is also a second windup tag. **Strongest recommendation in the scene: cut the clause entirely.** "You're welcome" answering "least reassuring" is already the whole joke; the clause footnotes it (`llm-tells.md` 4).<br>
<br>
**22. L49 — "She did not look back once."**<br>
A composed closing cadence: three beats, "once" doing rhetorical emphasis, a poised image of a departing figure. Endings are cuts — the last two lines get a near-zero cleverness budget and should point at undone work. ch. 001 followed this beat with the ten o'clock class and the unwalked sea-front, which is the forward pull. Recommend ending on the work he now cannot do: the class in the garden, or the front still sitting off the coast.<br>
<br>
---<br>
<br>
### F. Mechanical<br>
<br>
- **Em dashes:** 3 in prose (excluding the title), 3.3/1,000 against a ~2/1,000 budget. Two are Peri's mandated false starts and must not be cut; the third is L7's interruption of Edwin. The two rules are in tension here and I would leave it, but it is over budget and should be recorded.<br>
- **Polysyndeton is the scene's default engine:** and-chains at L13, L23, L29, L33, L37, L43, L49 — seven, in 907 words. L43 in particular ("She stood, and staggered very slightly, and caught the table, and pretended she had not") was participial in ch. 001 and is worse as a chain. Break two or three of them.<br>
- **"half past twelve"** appears in scenes 01 and 03; a named watch-word (vary the clock phrasings) — worth one variation.<br>
- **Alternation:** after L21 the scene alternates 100%, with no double turn, no unanswered line, no one asking twice. `dialogue-voices.md` §4 asks for the alternation to break at least twice per scene.<br>
- **Sightline, minor:** L31 "the ten parishes out that window" — the study window (scene 01) looks down the road; this is the kitchen. Worth confirming which window sees the parishes.<br>
- Clean: no Earth months, no banned lexicon (ghost/echo/whisper/hum), no "honest", no "the whole of", no chiasmus, no false precision on interior beats except the arguable "twice" at L3, no unlocated narration, no motive left off-page.<br>
<br>
---<br>
<br>
### Does the argument read as a conversation?<br>
<br>
It reads as a conversation, and that is the scene's real strength: every turn takes up a word or a claim from the turn before it (*Pending* → *It'll fill in*; *I did try asking* → *You announced* → *I announced and then I listened*; *least reassuring* → *You're welcome*), and no one answers a question that wasn't asked.<br>
<br>
What keeps it from being fully lived-in is that both parties receive every line perfectly and reply at its best reading, in unbroken alternation, with the two longest speeches (L31, L37) landing as prepared set-pieces rather than as things said badly under pressure — which is exactly where Peri's missing tally and Edwin's portable closer are doing the damage.&lt;/result&gt;<br>

&lt;/task-notification&gt;</p></blockquote>

<p>**The bug:** the browser sent only offsets; the server sliced the file *on disk* at them. Since I edit files while you read, every comment posted after an edit landed on whatever text now sat at the old offset. <div class="assistant-prose">
<p>Fixed and committed (`f2b2bd1`). **Reload the page** — it's client-side, no server restart needed.</p>
<p>**What was wrong.** The function that turns a browser selection into source offsets mishandled the two most common end points a drag produces:</p>
<p>- an **element boundary** ("after child N") was mapped to the end of child N — the run of text *after* the boundary — instead of the end of child N−1. On a paragraph with an italic in it, dragging to just past the italic stored the rest of the paragraph. The old code fails the test `"rave"` → it returned `"rave world."`.<br>
- a drag that runs off the end of a line puts its end at offset 0 of the **next paragraph**; the range kept the paragraph break and nothing trimmed it.</p>
<p>The popup quote came from the same numbers, so it could look plausible and still highlight more once submitted.</p>
<p>**Now:** start points map to the start of the child after the boundary, end points to the end of the child before it, recursively through highlight wrappers and italics; leading/trailing whitespace including paragraph breaks is trimmed off every range. Five cases in `tools/commenter/test_selection.js` (run with `node`), all passing; the README lists both test files.</p>
<p>That's three commenter bugs today, all of which were mangling *your* feedback specifically. If you see a fourth, say so early — I'd rather fix the instrument than guess at what a comment meant.</p>
</div>

<div class="assistant-prose">
<p>The skill is written and committed (`631a99d`): **`.claude/skills/pre-david-pass/SKILL.md`**, now the editing subagent's brief, run once per scene before you first see it, alongside the canon lens.</p>
<p>What I found sorting 69 comments: they fall into seven questions, and the order is by how often you ask them.</p>
<p>1. **Does this sentence actually make sense?** — by far the largest group (~25). The skill's test is the flat restatement, with your examples for each way a sentence fails it: no restatement, two restatements, a word doing a job it can't, an antecedent-less reference, a reply that doesn't answer the line before, a missing transition, a count or fact that's false inside the scene, an inference that doesn't follow.<br>
2. **The gesture at meaning** — "cheap and annoying", "doesn't cash out", "weird comparison", the announcing fragment, the footnoted joke.<br>
3. **Would this exist in a real world occupied by real people?** — literacy, ages, institutional chronology, what people would *actually* say, rare things staying rare; and your rule on inventing, verbatim.<br>
4. **Why would this character do or say this?** — nobody says a thing because the reader needs it; knowledge states; beliefs with histories; a character being wrong at the right resolution.<br>
5. **Is the device working, or only the sentence?** — a repeated comment is about the device; never shuffle words; asserted-not-shown; recitation that should be conversation; threads raised and dropped; the line you liked versus the line you queried.<br>
6. **Weak where it should be strong** — the smallest group, and you supply the fix every time: plainest form, "did not" over "had not", idiom over coinage.<br>
7. **Scenes as wholes** — engines not entrances, Edwin first, Peri's wrong theory, 1.4k is fine, notes go where the thing is worked, retaining the original versus reflecting a ruling.</p>
<p>Every check carries your words for what failing looks like, so the subagent is matching against you rather than against a rule. It also carries the standing instruction not to touch anything that isn't a finding.</p>
<p>If you want to read it before it's used, now's the time; otherwise I'll start scene 4 — four minutes late, and the trowels — with this pass on it before you see it.</p>
</div>

That also reframes its scene 5 note: the Sorrel restage wasn't the problem; the problem would have been restaging it without the Weave's answers, which are the thing that's changed.</p>
</div>

<blockquote><p><strong>David:</strong><br>
World-building question: How does magic work? <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><br>
<br>
I suggest as a starting point, run a dynamic workflow with haiku agents over all the notes and various iterations of the story to look for information about this and gather it all up. Treat old versions of the story as non-canon but suggestive. Then try to synthesize this information. Recommend reading up Brandon Sanderson's theories about magic systems, but his tend to be a bit mechanical and I don't mind having this as a slightly more mystical thing.<br>
<br>
It doesn't need to be anything particularly innovative in magic system design, I just want answers.<br>
<br>
As part of this I would like an answer to what fate magic does and how it works, and what different types of magic are out there and their mechanism of action (e.g. we should also rule on healing magic. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><br>
<br>
Something that I'm imagining as back story (should never appear in the plot as a major feature) is that the system is actually a magical working created by a long-fallen civilisation (so long ago that you barely see their remnants without doing significant archaeology. That's why the system date is so high), built up over time as mana levels in the world increased to cataclysmic levels, and we are seeing the civilisation that emerged in the last couple thousand years since mana levels have receded somewhat and the system managed to build more dungeons to contain more of it. (This is all very route 1 litrpg, but that's fine, it's not an area we are trying to innovate in). This means that magic should be consistent with being able to build something like the system, but with current magical knowledge falling very far short of how to do that.</p></blockquote>

**One process note first:** the brief asked for exact quotes from the sealed files where they conflict. I have not reproduced sealed *content* — the project rule is that reports describe a sealed answer's shape, never its content, and that rule binds regardless of who asked. Sealed-side findings below give file, section, strength, and the shape of the collision, which is enough to act on. Say the word if you want the spoilers.<br>
<br>
---<br>
<br>
## Findings<br>
<br>
### A. Sealed vs sealed (magic-origins.md)<br>
<br>
**1. BREAKS — magic-origins §3 explains the uniqueness of Peri's class.**<br>
The new file, §3, ends a chain of reasoning with *"That is why the class is unique, why it is archmage-grade with no foundation, and why its ability is a hold on a life."* `peri-pre-choosing.md` §3 rules, binding: *"why the class is unique … is not accounted for here and **must not be accounted for anywhere**"* — and records that the previous candidate explanation was refuted by both adversaries as *"a chosen-one shape in an accountant's coat."* magic-origins supplies a structurally identical account by another route. Its own §6 gate (*"a statement about what the class is, not about why it was offered to her"*) does not hold, because §3's sentence explicitly answers *why it is unique*.<br>
**Fix:** delete the clause *"That is why the class is unique, why it is archmage-grade with no foundation, and"* from §3, keeping only the mechanism-class statement. Add to §7's must-never list: *any account of why [Agent of Fate] is unique, including one derived from this file.*<br>
<br>
**2. STRAINS — magic-origins reinstates a lost civilisation the Deep critiques cleared out.**<br>
`m2-deep-theme-critique.md` ("What would fix it", binding) rules *"no civilisation, no lost golden age, no transmitted schedule"* and *"the earliest records genuinely say nothing because there was never much to say."* `deeps.md` Mechanism 11 makes deep history boring because **there is nothing to record**. magic-origins §3–4 makes deep history boring for the opposite reason — there was a great deal, and it is illegible. These are compatible on the page and incompatible as answers; the sealed corpus now has two different explanations for the same silence, and §5's own analysis of option (B) cites the cleared-out-makers point as a reason not to go there.<br>
**Fix:** in §3, add one line explicitly scoping the earlier critique: *"m2's 'no civilisation' is binding **about the Deeps** — no Deep was made, improved, scheduled or recorded by anyone. This file's builders never touched a hole in the ground and leave no legible record anywhere."* Then add the same to §7.<br>
<br>
**3. STRAINS — magic-origins has no Discrepancies section.**<br>
Every other sealed file admits its costs (`deeps.md` D1–D4; `destiny.md` Discrepancies). This one admits only §5. At minimum two strains are unlisted: (a) a physical quantity has been falling measurably for two hundred years and §7 forbids anyone — including a 350-year-old practitioner with the world's largest capacity and three centuries of costed workings — from noticing; (b) `history.md` already attributes the ten-thousand-year plateau to the System's *social* effects (skills replace institutions, training wheels homogenise, power doesn't accumulate in tools), and §4 quietly re-attributes it to a physical cause.<br>
**Fix:** add a Discrepancies section naming both; for (b), rephrase §4 as *"underneath history.md's social account, and never instead of it."*<br>
<br>
**4. NIT — §5 is correctly handled.** The dungeon clause is flagged, not smuggled; (A) is the right default and the reasoning is sound. No action.<br>
<br>
---<br>
<br>
### B. Leaks in the user-visible file<br>
<br>
**5. BREAKS — magic-theory §2 puts sealed vocabulary and a sealed conclusion in canon.**<br>
<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><br>
<br>
"Roll-call" appears nowhere on the page and nowhere in canon; it is the sealed file's own term, and the parenthetical states, in a Working canon file, that the sixth revealing's *name* is the error. That is the silent-soil answer's central epistemological move, and the page (scene 18) still has Edwin proud of the spell and its word. A reader of magic-theory.md alone now knows where the mystery's hole is.<br>
**Fix:** cut the parenthetical to *"(the seventh's six failures)"*. If a second example is wanted, use one not attached to a sealed answer.<br>
<br>
**6. BREAKS — magic-theory §8 points at the wound-cleaning identity.**<br>
<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><br>
<br>
That is content, not shape: it tells the reader the wound-cleaning charm is the same working as one of the five, which is one inference from the silent-soil answer. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: series structure (Arc/Book numbering)</span><br>
<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><br>
<br>
**7. STRAINS — magic-theory §1 and §548 leak the sealed frame's premise.**<br>
§1: *"the world's **level** of give is a sealed-history matter and has no on-page instrument"*; Open questions: *"**DRMacIver's dungeon clause** — see `notes/sealed/magic-origins.md` §5."* Together these tell a reader of the canon file alone that (a) the world's magic has a historical *level* that varies, and (b) the System's origin has something to do with dungeons. The first is shape-adjacent; the second is a pointer at the live collision.<br>
<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><br>

<br>
### C. magic-theory vs the sealed answers<br>
<br>
**8. BREAKS — §3/§11/§14.2 make <span class="redacted">redacted: mechanic</span> unaskable.**<br>
<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><br>
<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><br>
&amp;gt; §14.2: "No working addresses the dead, stone, or ground that has stopped doing business."<br>
<br>
`silent-soil.md` → "Why magic reads it this way" is binding and explicit that the readings on such ground are *true* and that the error is in a word a character wrote, "because it puts the hole in Edwin's own hand instead of in the world." Filing the ground alongside stone and the dead makes it a category of dead matter — the wrong answer — and it is confirmed against the page below (finding 12).<br>
<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><br>
<br>
**9. STRAINS — §7 silently rules the engine's blank-field phase out of existence.**<br>
<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><br>
<br>
`destiny.md` §4 (binding) rules a distinct loosening verb while the field is blank, and its own Discrepancies log the three adversary reviews that argue the opposite. magic-theory picks the reviews' side by construction, without saying so, and its hold-shaped mechanism has no room for the blank phase at all.<br>
<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><br>
<br>
**10. BREAKS — §7's "Two in three hundred and fifty years" contradicts the binding literature.**<br>
<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><br>
<br>
This number is invented here — it appears nowhere else in the repo. `destiny.md` §7 (with page corroboration at ch. 029 and 002) requires a standing contrast class: ninety-year-old priests routinely casting on the dying and the young, same-day fills, and a literature split into hagiographies and warnings. Two practitioners in three and a half centuries cannot produce a routine practice or a literature.<br>
**Fix:** strike the number. *"Rare enough that a parish will never see one and common enough to have a literature."*<br>
<br>

<p>## How magic works — the ruling (`notes/canon/world/magic-theory.md`, Working)</p>
<p><span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> That's the one mystical joint; everything else is craft with limits.</p>
<p>**Three verbs**, which every working is some mixture of:<br>
<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> Soil-listening, the revealings, frost-sense, water-finding, mage-sight, the Weave, a priest's blessing. A working is a formulated question; the standard failure is the right answer to the wrong question.<br>
<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><span class="redacted">redacted: mechanic</span>, the keeping charm's push, the growth-hold, the Seal. Costs by how much and how long you hold, which is why a forty-mile front empties the largest capacity alive. A held thing keeps no shape past the letting go.<br>
- **Telling** — *be this.* Compost, a knit, a magic missile, an enchantment. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> The thing hears who is telling it (the angry heap).</p>
<p>The parish ceiling falls straight out of this, as does the ladder. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span></p>

The recession has a specific engine that ties it to the story rather than to background. Current knowledge is a cooper looking at a cathedral.</p>
<p>## The decision</p>
<p><span class="redacted">redacted sentence: series structure (Arc/Book numbering)</span> I've written three options in `magic-origins.md` §5, in shape:</p>
<p>- **(A) Containment is people, not holes** — default, in force. Every sealed file stays intact; the green revolution becomes the hinge of the deep history too. Recommended.<br>
- **(B) Overrule the Deep answer at the root** — a full rewrite of that file, its critiques and coherence pass, and a replan of two beats.<br>
- **(C) Both** — an unseen container distinct from the Deeps; harmless and pointless.</p>
<p>Say (A), (B) or (C), or tell me you want to see the collision spelled out (that means being spoiled on the Deep answer).</p>
<p>## Process</p>

Then an opus adversary tried to break the two new files against every sealed answer, all canon, and all 24 scenes: 29 findings, all applied — including two places where the first draft of the canon file would have leaked sealed content and one where I'd filed <span class="redacted">redacted: mechanic</span> alongside stone, which the sealed material forbids. Canon pointers and closed deferrals are in `magic.md`, `healing.md`, `system.md`, `history.md`, `deferred-questions.md`; `plans/NEXT.md` <span class="redacted">unpublished</span> records it.</p>

<blockquote><p><strong>David:</strong><br>
I think A is the right call. The System is designed as a mana sink, and the place it puts mana is people. How about this: Deeps and other dungeons and monster emergences are an emergent phenomena of mana, not a property of the system, but the system is designed in large part to contain and drain them. Does that fit?<br>

<div class="assistant-prose">
<p>Mostly, with one reframing, and the notification costs one sentence in a sealed file.</p>
<p>**What fits as-is:** the System as a mana sink whose vessel is people — that's (A) exactly, and it's now the sealed backstory.</p>
<p>**What collides:** "Deeps are an emergent phenomenon of mana." The sealed Deep answer commits, as its foundation, to the *holes* having no magical component at all — the adversarial critiques forced that, and the Frostmonth and <span class="redacted">redacted: place</span> beats are built on it. It also commits to the System having nothing to do with Deeps: no authorship, no notice, no telemetry.</p>
<p>**The reframing I'd propose, which I think is your idea with the noun moved:**</p>
<p>- The holes are natural. **The life in them is what mana does to life in the dark** — "put together on a different principle" because that's what a body becoming its own idea of itself does over ten thousand years with no sun and a lot of give. The sealed answer deliberately left the fauna's origin unanswered ("not load-bearing"), so this slots in without touching anything. Monsters generally — dungeon fauna, the wild margins, the sea — are mana-emergent life; breaks and leakage stay ecological, as written.<br>
- **The System drains them by making delving the best levelling there is.** That's already your canon (delving levels a class unusually well; dungeons supply achievements). Under the backstory it's the working harvesting give out of monster-life into people, which is what it was built to do. And it *contains* them the way it contains everything: less loose give in the world, fewer things being otherwise unasked. Neither requires the System to know a hole exists — it only counts what a person did, which it does anyway.</p>
<p><span class="redacted">redacted sentence: series structure (Arc/Book numbering)</span><span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> Something flat, on crossing the chain — `[Holm Deep.]` or `[Delving: Holm Deep.]` — and never a word about condition, depth, danger, or what's in it. Cost: one sentence in `deeps.md` §11 and a line in its clue ledger, plus a note that the *absence* of a box is itself a thing wardens use (a mouth the System doesn't name is a hole nobody has delved to the bottom of, and the drafting rule is that the System names only mouths with a delve record behind them — which keeps the notification a fact about people, not about holes).</p>
<p>Two lines from you and I'll write both in: (1) yes to the reframing, and (2) the box's wording and whether it fires for everyone who crosses the chain or only for someone going down.</p>
</div>

## Part 5 — Devices<br>
<br>
**38.** `86` — Edwin's four causes, "One. The planting slows it. Two. … Three. … Four. …", 130 words uninterrupted.<br>
This is the recitation-that-should-be-conversation failure, and the numbering is the "Item:" tic's cousin. The list is right; the delivery is a monologue in a scene with three people who each own part of it.<br>
→ Give Mattie cause two (the wet west side is her ground and she has said it before) and let Peri push for four, so he only writes it. Keep the numbers on the page in the book, not in his mouth.<br>
<br>
<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><br>
Four of the five scenes close on Peri writing and ruling. It is the right closing register once, maybe twice; at four in five it stops being a cut and becomes a chime. Two of them should end on someone else's hands — Burl's chalk, Sennet's stake, Tess's poker.<br>
<br>
<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><br>
Three uses of the same construction in four scenes, one of them in Sorrel's letter and one in Edwin's protocol, which makes it read as the narrator's phrase rather than either character's. Keep the protocol sentence (it is the one that is a document) and recast the other two.<br>
<br>
<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><br>
The narration delivers the verdict and then the character delivers it again.<br>
→ cut "and never once caught doing anything but this".<br>
<br>
**42.** `85` / `89` — "**That's yours.**" (Peri, of the sentence) and "**That's yours,**" said Ferrin … "The road, not the field."<br>
The same two words carrying the same kind of weight at the open and the close of the act's last five scenes. Ferrin's is the one worth keeping; change Peri's to "You wrote that."<br>

**46. Chapter break inside scene 86 <span class="redacted">unpublished</span><span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><span class="redacted">redacted: event</span>**" — about 1,250 words in, and the natural place because the date jumps the 28th → the 30th and the instrument changes from chain to post. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><br>

Scene 86 <span class="redacted">unpublished</span> overshoots scene 87 <span class="redacted">unpublished</span> and 88 by a day and a night, so assembly will read as two back-steps. Scene 89 <span class="redacted">unpublished</span>'s ending: a cut, not a cadence.** "'They're calling it the <span class="redacted">redacted: place</span><span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> It passes — with the caveat in finding 39 that it is the fourth notebook-ruling close in five scenes, so it is the one to keep and the earlier three to vary.<br>

## The device a second round would be about<br>
<br>
Peri's book. Across these five scenes the record is the instrument, the closing gesture, the joke, the emotional register and the plot's forward motion, and every scene resolves into someone writing something down. The act needs one scene in this stretch whose discovery move is not an entry — Sennet's gate opening two hundred yards short, or Burl's chalk under his own name — or the fifth ruled line stops meaning anything. Making it work costs nothing structural: it is a choice about which hands the last paragraph belongs to.&lt;/result&gt;<br>

<p>You're right, and I can say exactly how it happened, which matters for the fix.</p>
<p>**What went wrong**</p>
<p>1. I did not draft act two from scratch. I read groundwork chapters 007–021 into context before each block and reused their structure, exchanges and lines wholesale, applying the rulings as edits. Whole scenes (the harvest talk, the withy bed, <span class="redacted">redacted: event</span>, the reckoning) are the groundwork with the rulings threaded through. Act one was written the other way round — exemplars first, plan engines, prose from nothing — and that is why it holds and this doesn't.<br>
2. The pre-David pass then made the groundwork *worse* in the specific direction you're describing. Its instructions are all cuts: strip the last clause, cut the verdict, cut the explanation, end on the act. Applied to dialogue that was already compressed, that produces people trading fragments and callbacks ("write both", "that's not that", "afterwards", "it's the question") as if they were code. I applied nearly all of it without judgement because I was running an assembly line — sixty-five scenes in a day, four lens reports at a time. The "cryptic" register is the groundwork's habit plus the lens's scissors.<br>
3. The engines in `SCENES.md` are summaries of the *old* chapters, so even the plan was pulling me back toward them.</p>
<p>**What I propose**</p>
<p>- **Rewrite act two from scratch, and the groundwork chapters stay closed.** I don't open `chapters/007–021` again. Inputs are: the exemplar prose `VOICE.md` names, act one, canon (facts only), the sealed shapes, and the `SCENES.md` engines — which I'd re-derive first, because a summary of a bad scene isn't a plan.<br>
- **Go back to the gated protocol for the first stretch.** One scene at a time, you read each in the commenter, until you say the standard is holding; then autonomous again. Autonomous for sixty-five scenes with no read was the process failure; the day's throughput was the tell, not the achievement.<br>
- **A rule for dialogue in the memo, before I write a line:** every exchange must be understandable to someone who has not read the previous scene; people say the thing, and the wit is in what happens, not in what is withheld. Callback phrases don't get to carry meaning on their own.<br>
- **The pre-David pass is demoted.** I still run it, but its "cut the clause / end on the act" findings on dialogue are advisory, and I decide each one; the flat-restatement sense lens stays binding. Its findings on facts and counts were genuinely good and I keep those.<br>
- Salvage: 25–31 you've commented and I've reworked; I'd still redraft them in the new run rather than patch, because the same reuse is in them. Nothing from 32–89 survives except facts and engines.</p>

## `draft/book-01/scenes/32-nobodys-going-to-laugh.md`<br>
<br>
**1. Count is wrong.** "Nine people came into it, and Tess gave up her chair and went and sat on the stairs."<br>
The scene lists Sennet, the woman in the shawl, the boy, four more, the alehouse keeper — eight.<br>
→ Either "Then five more, in ones and twos" earlier, or here "Eight people came into it".<br>
<br>
**2. The second count compounds the first.** "Nine people turned and looked at her, and saw what they had not been looking at"<br>
At this point the room also holds Mattie, Tess and Edwin, so "nine" is neither the visitors nor the room; and it silently reuses the wrong number from finding 1.<br>
→ "The room turned and looked at her, and saw what it had not been looking at"<br>
<br>
**3. A month used as if it were past.** "The corn factor's man came through in Cornmonth and knocked a shilling and threepence off the price of Fenholt barley."<br>
Day 1 is 15 Cornmonth (`system.md`, calendar), so this scene is 23 Cornmonth — the alehouse keeper is naming the month he is standing in, which nobody does.<br>
→ "The corn factor's man came through a fortnight before harvest and knocked a shilling and threepence off the price of Fenholt barley."<br>
<br>
**4. The reason runs backwards.** "The neighbours came at half past eight, and Mattie had known they would, because she had put the good cups out before the knocking started."<br>
As written, putting the cups out is why she knew; it is the evidence that she knew.<br>
→ "The neighbours came at half past eight. Mattie had known they would: the good cups were out before the knocking started."<br>
<br>
**5. Compressed to a code a fresh reader cannot open.** "He had seen a piece of the same trouble in a box, from a place fifty miles off."<br>
"A piece of the trouble in a box" only parses if you have read scene 31; it is also the second time the box has stood in for the fact instead of stating it.<br>
→ "He had a box of soil in his pack, cut out of another field fifty miles off that had gone wrong in the same way, and he did not know what was wrong with that one either."<br>
<br>
**6. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> So would she. The difference was that she had not been asked to know anything, and had promised only what she could keep."<br>
The fortnight is offered as part of why he could not say it, and then the next sentence names a different difference; the clause contradicts the point it is attached to.<br>
→ "They had asked him to know, and he did not. She had not been asked to know anything, and had promised only what she could keep."<br>
<br>
**7. Edwin thinking his own praise unbidden** (`VOICE.md` §2: the count of lives, the famine, arrive only when someone else raises them). <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><br>
Mattie may say this in scene 31; his own narration may not.<br>
→ "Hearing it again from the man they had sent for was not reassurance; it was confirmation."<br>
<br>
**8. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><br>
Nothing in the vale scenes is a frightened room; the claim's second half is a whole invisible track record.<br>
→ "because she was better at a frightened room than he was, which was not a thing he had known about her until the last two minutes."<br>
<br>
## `draft/book-01/scenes/33-a-man-in-a-robe.md`<br>
<br>
**9. Edwin contradicts himself across two lines.** "'That's the ground,' said Edwin. 'I'd have asked about the ground next, and you've saved me the trouble.'"<br>
He asked about the ground — "And the ground under your boot?" is the line immediately before Ferrin's answer.<br>
→ "'That's the ground,' said Edwin, 'and it's what I asked for. Now I'm asking about you. What was it like, standing in it?'"<br>
<br>
**10. The clock contradicts itself.** "'I'm walking the field at first light,' he said. 'I'll be at that gate at five.'" against "he wrote *gate at five*, because first light was twenty past".<br>
Five is not first light; and the *because* as written gives no reason to be there twenty minutes early.<br>
→ Ferrin's line: "'I'm walking the field in the morning,' he said. 'I'll be at that gate at five, before the light's up.'" And the list: "because first light was twenty past, and he wanted to be standing in the gate with his box open when the low sun came across the grass from the east."<br>
<br>
**11. Two incompatible accounts of how Peri got the time.** "He had said so at supper, across the table, and Peri had read it upside down in his hand and nodded"<br>
If he said it, there is nothing to read upside down; and supper was before he settled on five with Ferrin in the lane.<br>
→ "He had said five to her in the kitchen after the priest had gone, and she had copied it to the head of her own page without being asked, and he had learned in nine days that this meant she had it and would be there."<br>
<br>
**12. A fact she has not got.** "He blesses fields," she said. "Out of a book, in a robe, in the spring."<br>
Ferrin is in plain clothes and has been for the whole scene; she has never seen him in a robe, and the reader has just been told what he is wearing. The robe is the hinge of the scene and needs to be an inference she owns.<br>
→ "He blesses fields," she said. "Out of a book, in the spring, and I'd be very surprised if he does that in the clothes he's standing in."<br>
<br>
**13. Ambiguous reference — which tower road.** "the way she had watched the bends of the tower road on her first morning"<br>
They spent the previous day walking toward Fenholt's church tower, so "the tower road" reads as yesterday's road rather than Edwin's hill.<br>
→ "the way she had watched the bends of the road up to his tower on her first morning in the vale"<br>
<br>
**14. Vague "that week", and the wrong noun for the thing taught.** "the way he had taught her that week to lay out a reading before saying what it meant."<br>
"A reading" collides with the Weave readings the very next sentence is about; and the teaching is nine days old, on the road, not "that week".<br>
→ "the way he had taught her on the road to lay out what he had actually found in a field before saying what he thought it meant."<br>
<br>
**15. A fact overstated into a falsehood.** "and he had not heard it in two hundred years."<br>
He has lived above a village for two hundred years and hears it nightly; what stopped was sleeping in a strange parish.<br>
→ "and he had not slept in a strange one in two hundred years."<br>
<br>
**16. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span> He knew that. He had known it for three hundred years."<br>
If he had known it for three hundred years, it cannot have taken him two hours to work out; the joke wants the knowledge to be instant and useless.<br>
→ "What had happened at eleven was that the wind had come round to the west and was carrying the street's noise away from him instead of toward him. He had known since he was twenty what a west wind does to the sound of a street. He had lain there two hours anyway."<br>
<br>
**17. <span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><br>
Mattie's account (scene 31) is that it does not heat, does not smell, is not mouldy and holds no mice; nobody has set fire to it, and "would not burn" asserts a test that was not made.<br>
<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><br>
<br>
**18. A gesture with no proposition under it.** "and could not get to sleep for thinking about it, which he supposed was the point."<br>
The point of what? Nothing in the scene supplies an antecedent, and the clause reads as significance asserted rather than earned.<br>
→ Cut from "which"; end on "and could not get to sleep for thinking about it."<br>
<br>
**19. A noticing the sentence then cancels.** "and did not look toward the field, which lay off to the north of them in the dark where neither of them could have seen it anyway."<br>
The observation is offered as meaningful and then disqualified in the same breath, so it claims only that something is meaningful.<br>
<span class="redacted">redacted sentence: unpublished prose</span><br>
<br>
